Bers’ simultaneous uniformization and the intersection of Poincaré holonomy varieties
Shinpei Baba

Abstract
. We consider the space of ordered pairs of distinct ℂ ​ P 1 {\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1} -structures on Riemann surfaces (of any orientations) which have identical holonomy, so that the quasi-Fuchsian space is identified with a connected component of this space. This space holomorphically maps to the product of the Teichmüller spaces minus its diagonal. In this paper, we prove that this mapping is a complete local branched covering map. As a corollary, we reprove Bers’ simultaneous uniformization theorem without any quasi-conformal deformation theory. Our main theorem is that the intersection of arbitrary two Poincaré holonomy varieties ( SL 2 ⁡ ℂ \operatorname{SL}_{2}\mathbb{C} -opers) is a non-empty discrete set, which is closely related to the mapping.



Dedicated to Misha Kapovich on the occasion of his 60th birthday

1.
Introduction

In 1960, Bers established a bijection between pairs of Riemann surface structures of opposite orientations and typical discrete and faithful representations of a surface group into
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
up to conjugacy (
[
Ber60
]
).
It is called
Bers’ simultaneous uniformization theorem
, and it gave a foundation for the later evolutional development of the hyperbolic three-manifold theory by Thurston (
[
Thu81
]
) and many others.
In this paper, we partially generalize Bers’ theorem, in a certain sense, to generic surface representations into
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
, which are not necessarily discrete.

Throughout this paper, let
S
be a closed orientable surface of genus
g>1
.
Given a
quasi-Fuchsian
representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
, the domain of discontinuity is the union of disjoint topological open disks
\Omega^{+},\Omega^{-}
in
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
.
Then, their quotients
\Omega^{+}/{\rm Im}\rho,\Omega^{-}/{\rm Im}\rho
have marked Riemann surface structures with opposite orientations.

Let
S^{+},S^{-}
be
S
with opposite orientations.
Then Bers’ simultaneous uniformization theorem asserts that this correspondence gives a biholomorphism

(1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{QF}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{T}^{\ast}(=\mathbb{R}^{6g-6}\times\mathbb{R}^{6g-6})

where
\mathsf{QF}
is space of the quasi-Fuchsian representations
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
up to conjugation,
\mathsf{T}
is the Teichmüller space of
S^{+}
and
\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
is the Teichmüller space of
S^{-}
; see
[
Hub06
]

[
EK06
]
for the analyticity.
(Note that
\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
is indeed anti-holomorphic to
\mathsf{T}
; see
[
Wol10
]
. )

The
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
-character variety
of
S
is the space of homomorphisms
\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
, roughly, up to conjugation, and it has two connected components (
[
Gol88
]
).
Let
\textstyle\chi
denote the component consisting of representations
\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
which lift to
\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
; then
\textstyle\chi
strictly contains the (Euclidean) closure of
\mathsf{QF}
.

A
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure
on
S
is a locally homogeneous structure modeled on
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
, and its holonomy is in
\textstyle\chi
.
The quotients
\Omega^{+}/{\rm Im}\rho
and
\Omega^{-}/{\rm Im}\rho
discussed above have not only Riemann surfaces structures but also
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures on
S^{+}
and
S^{-}
, respectively.
In fact, almost every representation in
\textstyle\chi
is the holonomy of some
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure on
S

[
GKM00
]
; see §
2.1
for details.

In fact, each
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure on
S
corresponds to a holomorphic quadratic differential on a Riemann surface structure on
S
(§
2.1.2
).
Let
\mathsf{P}
be the space all (marked)
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures on
S^{+}
with the fixed orientation, which is identified with the cotangent bundle of
\mathsf{T}
.
Similarly, let
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
be the space of all marked
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
on
S^{-}
, identified with the cotangent bundle of
\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
.

By sending each quasi-Fuchsian representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
to the
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures
\Omega^{+}/{\rm Im}\rho
and
\Omega^{-}/{\rm Im}\rho
, the quasi-Fuchsian space
\mathsf{QF}
holomorphically embeds into
\mathsf{P}\times\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
as a closed half-dimensional submanifold.
The
holonomy map

\operatorname{Hol}\colon\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast}\to{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}

takes each
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure to its holonomy representation.
Now we introduce the space of all ordered pairs of distinct
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures sharing holonomy

\big\{(C,D)\in(\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast})^{2}\big|\operatorname{Hol}(C)=\operatorname{Hol}(D),C\neq D\big\}.

Let us denote this space by
\mathsf{B}
for appreciation of the work of Bers.
Since
\operatorname{Hol}
is locally biholomorphic,
\mathsf{B}
is also a half-dimensional closed holomorphic submanifold.
The map switching the order of
C
and
D
is a fixed-point-free biholomorphic involution of
\mathsf{B}
.
Then, the quasi-Fuchsian space
\mathsf{QF}
is biholomorphically identified with two connected components of
\mathsf{B}
, which are identified by this involution (Lemma
13.1
).
Every connected component of
(\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast})^{2}
contains at least one component of
\mathsf{B}
which does not correspond to
\mathsf{QF}
(see Lemma
2.5
).

Let
\psi\colon\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast}\to\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
be the projection from the space of all
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures on
S^{+}
and
S^{-}
to the space of all Riemann surface structures on
S^{+}
and
S^{-}
.
Define
\Psi\colon\mathsf{B}\to(\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast})^{2}\setminus\Delta
by
\Psi(C,D)=(\psi(C),\psi(D))
, where
\Delta
is the diagonal
\{(X,X)\mid X\in\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast}\}
(which can not intersect
\Psi(B)
).

It is a natural question to ask to what extent connected components of
\mathsf{B}
resemble the quasi-Fuchsian space
\mathsf{QF}
.
In this paper, we prove a local and a global property of the holomorphic map
\Psi
:

Theorem A
.

The map
\Psi
is a complete local branched covering map.

(For the definition of complete local branched covering maps, see §
2.5
.)
In particular,
\Psi
is open, and its fibers are discrete subsets of
\mathsf{B}
.
Thus its ramification locus is a nowhere-dense analytic subset, which may possibly be the empty set.
(The completeness of Theorem
A
is given by Theorem
12.2
, and the local property by Theorem
B
below.)

Note that, by the completeness in
A
, for every connected component
Q
of
\mathsf{B}
, the restriction
\Psi|Q
is surjective onto its corresponding component of
(\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast})^{2}\setminus\Delta
.
We also show that, towards the diagonal
\Delta
, the holonomy of
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures leaves every compact set in
\textstyle\chi
(see Proposition
12.6
).

The deformation theory of hyperbolic cone manifolds is developed, especially, by Hodgson, Kerckhoff and Bromberg
[
HK98
,
HK05
,
HK08
,
Bro04a
,
Bro04b
]
).
If cone angles exceed
2\pi
, their deformation theory is established only under the assumption that the cone singularity is short and, thus, the tube radius is large.
More generally, a conjecture of McMullen (
[
McM98
, Conjecture 8.1]
) asserts that the deformation space of geometrically-finite hyperbolic cone-manifolds is parametrized by using the cone angles and the conformal structures on the ideal boundary.
Theorem
A
provides some additional evidence for the conjecture, when the cone angles are
2\pi
-multiples (c.f.
[
Bro07
]
).

Bers’ simultaneous uniformization theorem is a consequence of the measurable Riemann mapping theorem.
It thus is important that the domain
\Omega^{+}\sqcup\Omega^{-}
is a (full measure) subset of
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
.
However, in general, developing maps of
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures are not embeddings, and Bers’ proof does not apply to the other components of
\mathsf{B}
.
In fact, Theorem
A
implies the simultaneous uniformization theorem (§
13
).
Thus we reprove Bers’ theorem genuinely from a complex analytic viewpoint,
without any quasi-conformal deformation theory.

Next we describe the local property in Theorem
A
.
Since
\operatorname{Hol}
is locally biholomorphic, for every
(C,D)\in\mathsf{B}
,
if an open neighborhood
V
of
(C,D)
in
\mathsf{B}
is sufficiently small, then
\operatorname{Hol}
embeds
V
onto a neighborhood
U
of
\operatorname{Hol}(C)=\operatorname{Hol}(D)
in
\textstyle\chi
.
Let
\mathsf{T}_{C}
and
\mathsf{T}_{D}
be
\mathsf{T}
or
\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
so that
\psi(C)\in\mathsf{T}_{C}
and
\psi(D)\in\mathsf{T}_{D}
, and define a holomorphic map
\Psi_{C,D}\colon U\to\mathsf{T}_{C}\times\mathsf{T}_{D}
by the restriction of
\Psi
to
V
and the identification
V\cong U
.
The following gives a finite-to-one “parametrization” of
U
by pairs of Riemann surface structures associated with
V
.

Theorem B
.

Let
(C,D)\in\mathsf{B}
.
Then, there is a neighborhood
V
of
(C,D)
in
\mathsf{B}
, such that

\operatorname{Hol}
embeds
V
into
\textstyle\chi
, and
the restriction of
\Psi
to
V
is a branched covering map onto its image in
T_{C}\times T_{D}

(Theorem
10.3
.)

By the simultaneous uniformization theorem,
for every
X\in\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
and
Y\in\mathsf{T}
,
the slices
\mathsf{T}\times\{Y\}
and
\{X\}\times\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
, called the
Bers’ slices
, intersect transversally in the point in
\mathsf{QF}
corresponding to
(X,Y)
by (
1
).
The Teichmüller spaces
\mathsf{T}
and
\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
are, as complex manifolds, open bounded pseudo-convex domains in
\mathbb{C}^{3g-3}
, where
g
is the genus of
S
.
In order to prove Theorem
A
and Theorem
B
, we consider the analytic extensions of
\mathsf{T}\times\{Y\}
and
\{X\}\times\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
in the character variety
\textstyle\chi
and analyze their intersection.

For each
X\in\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
, let
\mathsf{P}_{X}
be the space of all
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures on
X
.
Then
\mathsf{P}_{X}
is an affine space of holomorphic quadratic differentials on
X
, and thus
\mathsf{P}_{X}\cong\mathbb{C}^{3g-3}
.
Although the restrictions of the holonomy map
\operatorname{Hol}
to
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
are non-proper and non-injective, the restriction of
\operatorname{Hol}
to
\mathsf{P}_{X}
is a proper embedding (
[
Poi84
,
GKM00
]
, see also
[
Tan99
,
Kap95
,
Dum17
]
).
Let
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}=\operatorname{Hol}(\mathsf{P}_{X})
, which we shall call the
Poincaré holonomy variety
of
X
as its injectivity is due to Poincaré.
Note that, if
X\in\mathsf{T}
, then
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}
contains
\{X\}\times\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
as a bounded pseudo-convex subset, and similarly, if
Y\in\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
, then
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
contains
\mathsf{T}\times\{Y\}
as a bounded open subset.

The intersection theory of subvarieties and submanifolds in the character variety
\textstyle\chi
has been important (
[
Dum15
,
DW08
]

[
Fal83
, Theorem 12]
).
Since
\dim{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}
is half of
\dim{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
, it is a basic question to ask what the intersection of such smooth subvarieties looks like.

Theorem C
.

For all distinct
X,Y
in
\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
,
the intersection of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}
and
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
is a non-empty discrete set.

More precisely, we will show that
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
contains at least one point if the orientations of
X
and
Y
are the same, and at least two points if the orientations are opposite (Corollary
12.7
).
Such a global understanding of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
in Theorem
C
is completely new.
In fact, much of this paper is devoted to proving the discreteness of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
.

The deformation spaces,
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
, of
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures have two distinguished parametrizations: namely,
Schwarzian parametrization
(§
2.1.2
) and
Thurston parametrization
(§
2.1.6
).
In order to understand points in
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
, we give a comparison theorem between those two parametrizations.

Let
C
be a
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure on a Riemann surface
X
.
Then the quadratic differential of its Schwarzian parameters gives a vertical measured (singular) foliation
V
on
X
.
The Thurston parametrization of
C
gives the measured geodesic lamination
L
on the hyperbolic surface.
Dumas showed that
V
and
L

projectively
coincide in the limit as
C
leaves every compact set in
P_{X}
(
[
Dum06
,
Dum07
]
), see also
[
OSWW
]
.)

The measured geodesic lamination
L
of the Thurston parameter is also realized as a circular measured lamination
\mathcal{L}
on
C
, so that
\mathcal{L}
and
L
are the same measured lamination on
S
(§
2.1.7
).
In this paper, we prove a more explicit asymptotic relation between the Thurston lamination
\mathcal{L}
and the vertical foliation
V
, without projectivization.
For a quadratic differential
q=\phi\,dz^{2}
on a Riemann surface
X
, let
\|q\|=\int_{X}|\phi|\,dx\,dy
, the
L^{1}
-norm.
Then we have the following.

Theorem D
.

Let
X\in\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
.
For every
\epsilon>0
, there is
r>0
, such that, if the holomorphic quadratic differential
q
on
X
satisfies
\|q\|>r
, then, letting
C
be the
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure on
X
given by
q
, the vertical foliation
V
of
q
is
(1+\epsilon,\epsilon)
-quasi-isometric to
\sqrt{2}
times the Thurston lamination
\mathcal{L}
on
C
, up to an isotopy of
X
supported on the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of the zero set of
q
in the uniformizing hyperbolic metric on
X
.
(Theorem
4.1
.)

(See
4
for the definition of being quasi-isometric, and see §
2.1.7
for the Thurston lamination on a
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-surface.)

D
is reminiscent of the (refined) estimates of high energy harmonic maps between hyperbolic Riemann surfaces by Wolf (
[
Wol91
]
).

Last we address that, in our setting, a variation of McMullen’s conjecture can be stated in a global manner:

Question 1.1
.

For every (or even some) non-quasi-Fuchsian component
Q
of
\mathsf{B}
, is the restriction of
\Phi
to
Q
a biholomorphic mapping onto its corresponding component of
(\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast})^{2}
?

1.1.
Outline of this paper

In §
3
, we analyze the geometry of Epstein-Schwarz surfaces corresponding to
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures, using
[
Dum17
]
and
[
Bab
]
.
In §
4
, we analyze the horizontal foliations of
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures on
X
and
Y
corresponding to the intersection points of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
in Theorem
C
.
In fact, we show that such horizontal projectivized measured foliations projectively coincide towards infinity of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
(Theorem
4.5
).

A (fat) train-track is a surface obtained by identifying edges of rectangles in a certain manner.
In §
5
, we introduce more general train-tracks whose branches are not necessarily rectangles but more general polygons, cylinders, and even surfaces with staircase boundary (
surface train tracks
).
In §
6
, given a certain pair of flat surfaces, we decompose them into the surface train tracks in a compatible manner.

In §
7
, we prove Theorem
D
.
In §
8
, for every holonomy
\rho
in
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
outside a large compact subset
K
of
\textstyle\chi
, we construct certain surface train-track decompositions of
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures on
X
and
Y
corresponding to
\rho
in a compatible manner, using the decomposition of flat surfaces.
In §
9
, from the compatible decompositions of the
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures, we construct an integer-valued cocycle which changes continuously in
\rho\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}\setminus K
.
In §
10
, by this cocycle and some complex geometry, we prove the discreteness in Theorem
C
.
In §
12
, the completeness of Theorem
C
is proven.
In §
11
, we discuss the case when the orientations of
X
and
Y
are opposite.
In §
13
, we give a new proof of Bers’ theorem.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures

(General references are
[
Dum09
]
,
[
Kap01
, §7]
.)
Let
F
be a connected orientable surface.
A
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure
on
F
is a
({\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1},{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C}))
-structure.
That is, a maximal atlas of charts embedding open sets of
F
into
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
with transition maps in
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
.
Let
\tilde{F}
be the universal cover of
F
.
Then, equivalently, a
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure is a pair of

•

a local homeomorphism
f\colon\tilde{F}\to{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
and

•

a homomorphism
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})

such that
f
is
\rho
-equivariant (
[
Thu97
]
).
It is defined up to an isotopy of the surface and an element
\alpha
of
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
, i.e.
(f,\rho)\sim(\alpha f,\alpha^{-1}\rho\alpha)
.
The local homeomorphism
f
is called the
developing map
and the homomorphism
\rho
is called the
holonomy representation
of a
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure.
We also write the developing map of
C
by
\operatorname{dev}C
.

2.1.1.
The holonomy map

The
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
-
character variety
of
S
is the space of the equivalence classes homomorphisms

\{\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C}))\}\sslash{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C}),

where the quotient is the GIT-quotient (see
[
New
]
for example).
For the holonomy representations of
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures on
S
, the quotient is exactly given by the conjugation by
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
.
Then, the character variety has exactly two connected components, distinguished by the lifting property to
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
; see
[
Gol88
]
.
Let
\textstyle\chi
be the component consisting of representations which lift to
\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
, and
let
\mathsf{P}
be the space of marked
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures on
S
.
Then the
holonomy map

\operatorname{Hol}\colon\mathsf{P}\to{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}

takes each
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure to its holonomy representation.
Then
\operatorname{Hol}
is a locally biholomorphic map, but not a covering map onto its image (
[
Hej75
,
Hub81
,
Ear81
]
).
By Gallo, Kapovich, and Marden (
[
GKM00
]
),
\rho\in{\rm Im}\operatorname{Hol}
if and only if
\rho
is non-elementary and
\rho
has a lift to
\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
.
In particular,
\operatorname{Hol}
is almost onto
\textstyle\chi
.

2.1.2.
The Schwarzian parametrization

(See
[
Dum09
]

[
Leh87
]
.)
Let
X
be a Riemann surface structure on
S
.
Then, the hyperbolic structure
\tau_{X}
uniformizing
X
is, in particular, a
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure on
X
.
For an arbitrary
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure
C
on
X
, the Schwarzian derivative gives a holomorphic quadratic differential on
X
by comparing with
\tau_{X}
, so that
\tau_{X}
corresponds to the zero differential.
Then
(X,q)
is the
Schwarzian parameters
of
C
.
Let
\mathsf{QD}(X)
be the space of the holomorphic quadratic differentials on
X
, which is a complex vector space of dimension
3g-3
.
Thus, the space
\mathsf{P}_{X}
of all
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
structures on
X
is identified with
\mathsf{QD}(X)
.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
Poi84
,
Kap95
]
, see also
[
Tan99
,
Dum17
]
)
.

For every Riemann surface structure
X
on
S
,
the set
\mathsf{P}_{X}
of projective structures on
X
is property embedded in
\textstyle\chi
by
\operatorname{Hol}
.

For
X\in\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
, let
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}
denote the smooth analytic subvariety
\operatorname{Hol}({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X})
.
Pick any metric
d
on
\mathsf{T}
and
\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
compatible with their topology (for example, the Teichmüller metric or the Weil-Peterson metric).

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
B
be an arbitrary bounded subset of either
\mathsf{T}
or
\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
.
For every compact subset
K
in
\textstyle\chi
, there is
\epsilon>0
, such that, if distinct
X,Y\in B
satisfy
d(X,Y)<\epsilon
, then
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}\cap K=\emptyset
.

Proof.

For each
X\in\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
, by Theorem
2.1
,
\mathsf{P}_{X}
is properly embedded in
\textstyle\chi
.
For a neighborhood
U
of
X
, let
D_{r}(U)
denote the set of all holomorphic quadratic differentials
q
on Riemann surfaces
Y
in
U
such that the
L^{1}
-norm
\|q\|
is less than
r
.
Since
\operatorname{Hol}
is a local biholomorphism, for every
X\in\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there is a neighborhood
U
of
X
,
\operatorname{Hol}
embeds
D_{r}(U)
into
\textstyle\chi
.
Let
\mathsf{P}_{U}
be the space of all
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures whose complex structures are in
U
.
Then, if
r>0
is sufficiently large, we can, in addition, assume that
K\cap\operatorname{Hol}(\mathsf{P}_{U})=K\cap\operatorname{Hol}(D_{r}(U))
.
Therefore, for all
Y,W\in U
, we have
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{W}\cap K=\emptyset
.
∎

2.1.3.
Singular Euclidean structures

(See
[
Str84
]
,
[
FM12
]
.)
Let
q=\phi\,dz^{2}
be a quadratic differential on a Riemann surface
X
.
Then
q
induces a singular Euclidean structure
E
on
S
from the Euclidean structure on
\mathbb{C}
: Namely, for each non-singular point
z\in X
, we can identify a neighborhood
U_{z}
of
z
with an open subset of
\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{E}^{2}
by the integral

\eta(w)=\int_{z}^{w}\sqrt{\phi}\,dz

along a path connecting
z
and
w
, where
w\in U_{z}
is a fixed base point
(for details, see
[
Str84
]
).
Then the zeros of
q
correspond to the singular points of
E
.
Note that, for
r>0
, if the differential
q
is scaled by
r
, then the Euclidean metric
E
is scaled by
\sqrt{r}
.
Let
E^{1}
denote the normalization
\frac{E}{{\rm Area}E}
of
E
by the area.

The complex plane
\mathbb{C}
is foliated by horizontal lines and, by the identification
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{E}^{2}
, the vertical length
dy
gives a canonical transversal measure to the foliation.
Similarly,
\mathbb{C}
is also foliated by the vertical lines, and the horizontal length
dx
gives a canonical transversal measure to the foliation.
Then, those vertical and horizontal foliations on
\mathbb{C}
induce vertical and horizontal singular foliations on
E
which meet orthogonally.

In this paper, a
flat surface
is the singular Euclidean structure obtained by a quadratic differential on a Riemann surface, which has vertical and horizontal foliations.

2.1.4.
Measured laminations

( See
[
Thu81
,
EM87
]
for details)
Let
\sigma
be a hyperbolic structure on the closed surface
S
.
A
geodesic lamination
on
\sigma
is a set of disjoint geodesics whose union is a closed subset of
S
.
A
measured (geodesic) lamination

L
on
\sigma
is a pair of a geodesic lamination and its transversal measure.
In this paper, for an arc
\alpha
on
\sigma
transversal to
L
, we denote, by
L(\alpha)
, the transversal measure of
\alpha
given by
L
.
If we take a different hyperbolic structure
\sigma^{\prime}
on
S
, there is a unique geodesic representative on
L
on
\sigma^{\prime}
.
We thus can define measured laminations without fixing a specific hyperbolic structure on
S
.

2.1.5.
Bending a geodesic in the hyperbolic three-space

The following well-known lemma describes a closeness of a geodesic and a piecewise geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
with a small amount of bending.

Lemma 2.3
.

(
[
CEG87
, Theorem I.4.2.10]
)
Let
c\colon[0,\ell]\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
be a piecewise geodesic parametrized by arc length.
Let
s(t)
be the geodesic segment in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
connecting
c(0)
to
c(t)
.
Let
\theta(t)
be the angle between the forward tangent vector of
c
at
t
and the forward tangent vector of
s(t)
at
c(t)
.

For every
\epsilon>0
and
r>0
, there is
\delta>0
such that, if each smooth geodesic segment of
c
has length at least
r
and the exterior angle of
c
at every singular point of
c
is less than
\delta
, then
\theta(t)<\epsilon
for all
t\in[0,\ell]
.

2.1.6.
Thurston’s parameterization

By the uniformization theorem of Riemann surfaces,
the space of all marked hyperbolic structures on
S
is identified with the space
\mathsf{T}
of all marked Riemann surface structures.
Let
\mathsf{ML}
be the space of measured laminations on
S
.
Note that
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
is the ideal boundary of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, so that
\operatorname{Aut}{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}={\rm Isom}^{+}\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
In fact, Thurston gave a parameterization of
\mathsf{P}
using the three-dimensional hyperbolic geometry.

Theorem 2.4

(Thurston, see
[
KP94
,
KT92
]
)
.

There is a natural (tangential) homeomorphism

\mathsf{P}\to\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{ML}.

Suppose that, by this homeomorphism,
C=(f,\rho)\in\mathsf{P}
corresponds to a pair
(\sigma,L)\in\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{ML}
.
Let
\tilde{L}
be the
\pi_{1}(S)
-invariant measured lamination on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
obtained by lifting
L
.
Then
(\sigma,L)
yields a
\rho
-equivariant pleated surface
\beta\colon\mathbb{H}^{2}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
, obtained by bending
\mathbb{H}^{2}
along
\tilde{L}
by the angles given by its transversal measure.
The map
\beta
is called a
bending map
, and it is unique up to post-composing with
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
.

2.1.7.
Collapsing maps

(
[
KP94
]
; see also
[
Bab20
]
.)
Let
C\cong(\tau,L)
be a
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure expressed in Thurston parameters.
Let
\tilde{C}
be the universal cover of
C
.
Then
\tilde{C}
can be regarded as the domain of
f
, so that
\tilde{C}
is holomorphically immersed in
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
.
A
round disk
is a topological open disk whose development is a round disk in
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
, and a
maximal disk
is a round disk which is
not
contained in a strictly bigger round disk.
In fact, for all
z\in\tilde{C}
, there is a unique maximal disk
D_{z}
whose core contains
z
.
Then there is a measured lamination
\mathcal{L}
on
C
obtained from the cores of maximal disks in the universal cover
\tilde{C}
, such that
\mathcal{L}
is equivalent to
L
in
\mathsf{ML}
.
This lamination is the
Thurston lamination
on
C
.
In addition, there is an associated continuous map

\kappa\colon C\to\tau
which takes
\mathcal{L}
to
L
, called
the collapsing map
.

Then, the bending map and the developing of
C
are related by the collapsing map
\kappa
and appropriate nearest point projections in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
:
Let
\tilde{\kappa}\colon\tilde{C}\to\mathbb{H}^{2}
be the lift of
\kappa
to a map between the universal covers.
Let
H_{z}
be the hyperbolic plane in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
bounded by the boundary circle of
D_{z}
.
There is a unique nearest point projection from
D_{z}
to
H_{z}
.
Then
\beta\circ\tilde{\kappa}(z)
is the nearest point projection of
f(z)
to
H_{z}
.

2.2.
Bers’ space

Recall, from §
1
, that
\mathsf{B}
is the space of ordered pairs of
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures on
S
with identical holonomy, which may have different orientations.

Lemma 2.5
.

Every component of
(\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast})^{2}

contains, at least, one connected component of
\mathsf{B}
which is not identified with the quasi-Fuchsian space.

Proof.

By
[
GKM00
]
,
every non-elementary representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
is the holonomy representation of
infinitely many
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures on
S^{+}
whose developing maps are not embedding, and also of
infinitely many
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures of
S^{-}
whose developing maps are not embedding.
Therefore,
since a quasi-Fuchsian component of
\mathsf{B}
consists of pairs of
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures whose developing maps are embedding,
every component of
(\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast})^{2}
contains at least one connected component of
\mathsf{B}
, which is not a quasi-Fuchsian component.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

\mathsf{B}
is a closed analytic submanifold of
\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
of complex dimension
6g-6
.

Proof.

It is a holomorphic submanifold, since
\operatorname{Hol}\colon\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast}\to{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
is a local biholomorphism.
As
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}=6g-6
, the complex dimension of
\mathsf{B}
is also
6g-6
.
Let
(C_{i},D_{i})
be a sequence in
\mathsf{B}
converging to
(C,D)
in
(\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast})^{2}
.
Then, since
\operatorname{Hol}C_{i}=\operatorname{Hol}D_{i}
, by the continuity of
\operatorname{Hol}
,
\operatorname{Hol}(C)=\operatorname{Hol}(D)
.
Therefore
\mathsf{B}
is closed.
∎

2.3.
Angles between laminations

Let
F
be a surface with a hyperbolic or singular Euclidean metric.
Let
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
be (non-oriented) geodesics on
F
with non-empty intersection.
Then, for
p\in\ell_{1}\cap\ell_{2}
, let
\angle_{p}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\in[0,\pi/2]
denote the
angle
between
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
at
p
.

Let
L_{1}

L_{2}
be geodesic laminations or foliations on
F
.
Then

\angle(L_{1},L_{2})
be the infimum of
\angle_{p}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\in[0,\pi/2]
over all
p\in L_{1}\cap L_{2}
where
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are leaves of
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, respectively, containing
p
.
By convention, if
L_{1}\cap L_{2}=\emptyset
, then
\angle(L_{1},L_{2})=0
.
We say that
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are
\epsilon
-parallel
, if
\angle(L_{1},L_{2})<\epsilon
.

2.4.
The Morgan-Shalen compactification

(See
[
CS83
,
MS84
]
, see also
[
Kap01
, §10.3]
.)
The Morgan-Shalen compactification is a compactification of
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
-character variety, introduced in
[
CS83
,
MS84
]
.
For our
\textstyle\chi
, each boundary point corresponds to a minimal action of
\pi_{1}(S)
on a
\mathbb{R}
-tree,
\pi_{1}(S)\curvearrowright T
.

Every holonomy
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
induces a translation length function
\rho^{\ast}\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and a minimal action
\pi_{1}(S)
on a
\mathbb{R}
-tree also induces a translation length function.
Then
\rho_{i}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
converges to a boundary point
\pi_{1}(S)\curvearrowright T
if the length function
\rho^{\ast}_{i}
projectively converges to the projective class of the translation function of
\pi_{1}(S)\curvearrowright T
as
i\to\infty
.

2.5.
Complex geometry

We recall some basic complex geometry used in this paper.
Let
U,W
be complex manifolds of the same dimension.
A holomorphic map
\phi\colon U\to W
is a
(finite) branched covering map
if

•

there are closed analytic subsets
U’,W^{\prime}
of dimensions strictly smaller than
\dim U=\dim W
, such that the restriction of
\phi
to
U\setminus U^{\prime}
is a covering map onto
W\setminus W^{\prime}
, and

•

its covering degree is finite. (See
[
FG02
, p227]
.)

A holomorphic map
\phi\colon U\to W
is a
local branched covering map
if, for every
z\in U
, there is a neighborhood
V
of
z
in
U
such that the restriction
\phi|V
is a branched covering map onto its image.
A holomorphic map
U\to W
is
complete
if it has the (not necessarily unique) path lifting property (
[
AS60
]
).

Let
U
be an open subset of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Then a subset
V
of
U
is
analytic
if it is locally an intersection of zeros of finitely many holomorphic functions.

Proposition 2.7

(Proposition 6.1 in
[
FG02
]
)
.

Every connected bounded analytic set in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is a discrete set.

Theorem 2.8

(p107 in
[
GR84
]
, Theorem 7.9 in
[
HY99
]
)
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be a region.
Suppose that
f\colon U\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
is a holomorphic map with discrete fibers.
Then it is an open map.

3.
Approximations of Epstein-Schwarz surfaces

3.1.
Epstein surfaces

(See Epstein
[
Eps
]
, and also Dumas
[
Dum17
]
.)
Let
C
be a
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure on
S
.
Fix a developing pair
(f,\rho)
of
C
, where
f\colon\tilde{C}\to{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
is the developing map and
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
is the holonomy representation, which is unique up to
{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
.
For
z\in\mathbb{H}^{3}
, by normalizing the ball model of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
so that
z
is the center, we obtain a spherical metric
\nu_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(z)
on
\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{3}={\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
.

Given a conformal metric
\mu
on
C
, there is a unique map
\operatorname{Ep}\colon\tilde{C}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
such that, for each
x\in\tilde{C}
, the pull back of
\nu_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\operatorname{Ep}(z)
coincides with
\tilde{\mu}
at
z
.
This map is
\rho
-equivariant, and called the
Epstein surface
.

3.2.
Approximation

Let
C=(X,q)
be a
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure on
S
expressed in Schwarzian coordinates, where
q
is a holomorphic quadratic differential on a Riemann surface
X
.
Then
q
yields a flat surface structure
E
on
S
.
Moreover
q
gives a vertical measured foliation
V
and a horizontal measured foliation
H
on
E
.

Let
\operatorname{Ep}\colon\tilde{S}\to\mathbb{H}^{3}
be the Epstein surface of
C
with the conformal metric given by
E
.
Then, let
\operatorname{Ep}^{\ast}\colon T\tilde{S}\to T\mathbb{H}^{3}
be the derivative of
\operatorname{Ep}
, where
T\tilde{S}
and
T\mathbb{H}^{3}
denote the tangent bundles.
Let
d\colon\tilde{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be the distance function from the singular set
\tilde{Z}_{q}
with respect to the singular Euclidean metric of
\tilde{E}
.

Let
v^{\prime}(z)
be the vertical unit tangent vector of
\tilde{E}
at a smooth point
z
.
Similarly, let
h^{\prime}(z)
be the horizontal unit tangent vector at a smooth point
z
of
\tilde{E}
.

Lemma 3.1

(
[
Eps
]
, Lemma 2.6 and Lemma 3.4 in
[
Dum17
]
)
.

(1)

\|\operatorname{Ep}^{\ast}h^{\prime}(z)\|<\frac{6}{d(z)^{2}}
;

(2)

\sqrt{2}<\|\operatorname{Ep}^{\ast}v^{\prime}(z)\|<\sqrt{2}+\frac{6}{d(z)^{2}}
;

(3)

h^{\prime}(z),v^{\prime}(z)
are principal directions of
\operatorname{Ep}
at
z
;

(4)

k_{v}<\frac{6}{d(z)^{2}}
, where
k_{v}
is the principal curvature of
\operatorname{Ep}
in the vertical direction.

Consider the Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{E}^{2}
.
By the exponential map
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
, we push forward a complete Euclidean metric to
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
, which is invariant under the action of
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
.
If a simply connected region
Q
in the flat surface
E
contains no singular points, then
Q
is immersed into
\mathbb{C}
locally isometrically preserving horizontal and vertical directions.
Using Lemma
3.1
and the definition of Epstein surfaces, one obtains the following.

Lemma 3.2
.

(
[
Bab
, Lemma 12.15]
.)
For every
\epsilon>0
, there is
r>0
, such that if
Q
is a region in
E
satisfying

•

Q
has
E
-diameter less than
r
, and

•

the distance from the singular set of
E
is more than
r
.

then
\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
and the developing map are
\epsilon
-close pointwise with respect to the complete Euclidean metrics.

We shall further analyze vertical curves on Epstein surfaces.
Let
v\colon[0,\ell]\to\tilde{E}
be a path in a vertical leaf, such that
v
contains no singular point and has a constant speed
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
in the Euclidean metric.
Let
\operatorname{Ep}^{\perp}(z)
be the unit normal vector of the Epstein surface
\operatorname{Ep}
at each smooth point
z\in\tilde{E}
.
Let
s_{t}
be the geodesic segment in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
connecting
\operatorname{Ep}v(0)
to
\operatorname{Ep}v(t)
; see
Figure

1
.

\begin{overpic}[Figure/AlmostGeodesic] \put(80.0,24.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\small$\operatorname{Ep}^{\ast}v^{\prime}(t)$}}
\put(42.0,13.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$s_{t}$}}
\put(17.0,1.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\small$\operatorname{Ep}v(0)$}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 1.

then the total curvature along

The following lemma is an analogue of
Lemma

2.3
regarding piecewise geodesic curves for smooth curves.

Lemma 3.3
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there is (large)
\omega>0
only depending on
\epsilon
, such that, w.r.t. the
E
-metric, if the distance of the vertical segment
v
from the zeros
Z_{q}
of
q
is more than
\omega
,
then the angle between
\operatorname{Ep}^{\ast}v^{\prime}(t)
and the geodesic containing
s_{t}
is less than
\epsilon
for all
t
. (Figure
1
.)

Proof.

In fact, the proof of this lemma is essentially reduced to the analogous lemma (
Lemma

2.3
) for piecewise geodesic curves as follows.

Fix a Riemannian metric on the tangent bundle of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
which is invariant under the isometries of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Then, by Lemma
3.1
(
2
) and (
4
), for every
\epsilon_{1}>0
, there is sufficiently large
\omega>0
such that, if a vertical segment
v\colon[0,\ell]\to\tilde{E}
of unit speed has length less than
\frac{1}{\epsilon}
and distance from
Z_{p}
at least
\omega
, then the smooth curve
\operatorname{Ep}\circ\,v
is
\epsilon_{1}
-close to the geodesic segment connecting the endpoints of
\operatorname{Ep}\circ\,v
in the
C^{1}
-topology with respect to the invariant metric.
Therefore, the lemma holds true under the additional assumption that the length of
v
is uniformly bounded from above.

Now, without any upper bound on the length, let
v\colon[0,\ell]\to\tilde{E}
be a vertical segment of unit speed which has distance at least
\omega
from
Z_{p}
.
Let
\epsilon_{1}>0
be a constant.
Then we decompose
v
into
n
segments
v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n}
so that the first
n-1
segments
v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n-1}
have length exactly
\frac{1}{\epsilon_{1}}
and the last segment
v_{n}
has length at most
\frac{1}{\epsilon_{1}}
.
For all
i=1,2,\dots,n
, let
u_{i}
be the geodesic segment connecting the endpoints of
\operatorname{Ep}\circ v_{i}
.
Then, by the argument above, for every
\epsilon_{2}>0
, if
\epsilon_{1}>0
is sufficiently small, then the piecewise geodesic curve
\cup_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}
is
\epsilon_{2}
-close to
\operatorname{Ep}\circ v
in
C^{1}
-topology.
We can, in addition, assume that the exterior angle at the common endpoint of
u_{i}
and
u_{i+1}
is less than
\epsilon_{2}
for all
i=1,2,\dots,n_{1}
.
Therefore, by
Lemma

2.3
, for every
\epsilon_{2}>0
, if
\epsilon_{1}>0
is sufficiently small, then the piecewise geodesic curve
\cup_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}
is
\epsilon_{2}
-close to the geodesic segment connecting the endpoints of
\operatorname{Ep}\circ v
in
C^{1}
-topology.
(See
Figure

2
.)

Therefore, for every
\epsilon>0
, if
\epsilon_{1}>0
is sufficiently small, then
\operatorname{Ep}\circ v
is
\epsilon
-close to the geodesic segment connecting its endpoints.
Then the lemma immediately follows.
