Train-track graphs for non-transversal branches

Let
P_{X}
and
P_{Y}
be the corresponding branches of
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
and
\mathbf{T}_{Y,\rho}
, respectively, which are non-transversal.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{X}
and
\mathcal{P}_{Y}
be the branches of
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
and
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
corresponding to
P_{X}
and
P_{Y}
, respectively.
Then, by Theorem
8.2
,
\hat{\beta}_{X,\rho}|\partial\mathcal{P}_{X}
is
\epsilon
-close to
\hat{\beta}_{Y,\rho}|\partial\mathcal{P}_{Y}
in the
C^{0}
-metric and
C^{1}
-close along the vertical edges.
Let
\sigma_{X}
be a pleated surface with crown-shaped boundary whose truncation approximates
\hat{\beta}_{X}|\mathcal{P}_{X}
, and let
\sigma_{Y}
be the pleated surfaced boundary with crown-shaped boundary whose truncation approximates
\hat{\beta}_{Y}|\mathcal{P}_{Y}
, such that
\sigma_{X}
and
\sigma_{Y}
share their boundary (in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
).
In particular, the base hyperbolic surfaces for
\sigma_{X}
and
\sigma_{Y}
are diffeomorphic preserving markings and spikes.

Let
\nu_{X}
and
\nu_{Y}
be the bending measured laminations for
\sigma_{X}
and
\sigma_{Y}
, respectively; then
\nu_{X}
and
\nu_{Y}
contain only finitely many leaves whose connect ideal points.

In Thurston coordinates, the developing map and the pleated surface of a
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure are related by the nearest point projection to the supporting planes of the pleated surface (
[
KP94
,
Bab20
]
).
For small
\upsilon>0
,
let
F_{X}
,
F_{Y}
be the surfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
which are at distance
\upsilon
from
\sigma_{X}
and
\sigma_{Y}
in the direction of the nearest point projections of
P_{X}
and
P_{Y}
(
[
EM87
, Chapter II.2]
).

Thus, if necessary, refining
\nu_{X}
to
\nu_{Y}
to ideal triangulations of the hyperbolic surfaces appropriately, pick an (irreducible) sequence of flips
w_{i}
which connects
\nu_{X}
to
\nu_{Y}
.
Clearly the sequence
w_{i}
corresponds to a sequence of triangulations.

Lemma 9.13
.

If a bounded subset
K\subset{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
is sufficiently large, for every
\rho\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}\setminus K
and all non-transversal branches
P_{X}
and
P_{Y}
, there is a uniform upper bound on the length of the flip sequence
which depends only on
X,Y\in\mathsf{T}
, or appropriate refinements into triangulations.

Proof.

This follows from the length bound in Proposition
6.2
.
∎

A triangulation in the sequence given by
w_{i}
is
realizable
, if there is an equivariant pleated surface homotopic to
\sigma_{X}
(and
\sigma_{Y}
) relative to the boundary such that the pleating locus agrees with the triangulation.
In general, a triangulation in the sequence is not realizable when the endpoints of edges develop to the same point on
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
.
However a generic perturbation makes the triangulation realizable:

Lemma 9.14
.

For almost every perturbation of the holonomy of
P_{X}
and holonomy equivariant perturbation of the (ideal) vertices of
\tilde{\sigma}_{X}
(and
\tilde{\sigma}_{Y}
) in
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
, all triangulations in the flip sequence
w_{i}
are realizable.
Moreover, the set of realizable perturbations is connected.

Proof.

If
\sigma_{X}
is an ideal polygon, the holonomy is trivial.
Then, since there are only finitely many vertices and
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
has real dimension two, almost every perturbation is realizable.

If
\sigma_{X}
is not a polygon,
an edge of a triangulation forms a loop if the endpoints are at the same spike of
\tau_{X}
.
For each loop
\ell
of
\tau_{X}
, the condition that the holonomy of
\ell
is the identity is a complex codimension, at least, one in the character variety (and also in the representation variety).
Since the flip sequence is finite, for almost all perturbations of the holonomy, if an edge of a triangulation in the sequence forms a loop, then its holonomy is non-trivial.
Clearly, such a perturbation is connected.
Then, for every such perturbation of the holonomy, it is easy to see that, for almost all equivariant perturbations of the ideal points, the triangulations in the sequence are realizable.
∎

For every perturbation of the holonomy and the ideal vertices given by Lemma
9.14
, the flip sequence
w_{i}
gives
the sequence of pleated surfaces
\sigma_{X}=\sigma_{1}\xrightarrow{w_{1}}\sigma_{2}\xrightarrow{w_{2}}\dots\xrightarrow{w_{n-1}}\sigma_{n}=\sigma_{Y}
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
connecting
\sigma_{Y}
to
\sigma_{X}
, such that
\sigma_{i}
’s share their boundary geodesics and ideal vertices.

\begin{overpic}[Figure/HomotopyBetweenPleatedSurfaces] \end{overpic}

Figure 25.
The blue lines are the pleating lamination interpolating the pleated surfaces which differ by a flip.

For each flip
w_{i}
, the pairs of triangles of the adjacent pleated surfaces
\sigma_{i}
and
\sigma_{i+1}
bound a tetrahedron in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
To be precise, if the four vertices are contained in a plane, the tetrahedron is collapsed into a quadrangle, but it does not affect the following argument.
The edges exchanged by the flip correspond to the opposite edges of the tetrahedron.
Then pick a geodesic segment connecting those opposite edges.
Then there is a path
\sigma_{t}\penalty\ (i\leq t\leq i+1)
of pleated surfaces with a single cone point of angle more than
2\pi
such that

•

\sigma_{t}
connects
\sigma_{i}
to
\sigma_{i+1}
;

•

the pleated surfaces
\sigma
share their quadrangular boundary, which corresponds to the ideal quadrangle supporting the flip
w_{i}
;

•

by the homotopy,
\sigma_{t}
sweeps out the tetrahedron;

•

the cone point on the geodesic segment (see Figure
25
).

In this manner, this sequence of pleated surfaces
\sigma_{i}
continuously extends to a homotopy of the pleated surfaces with, at most, one singular point of cone angle greater than
2\pi
.
This interpolation also connects a bending cocycle on
\sigma_{i}
to a bending cocycle on
\sigma_{i+1}
continuous, although the induced cocycle on
\sigma_{i+1}
may correspond to a measured lamination only immersed on the surface, since the edges of the triangulations transversally intersect.
Thus,
\nu_{X}
induces a sequence of the bending (immersed) measured laminations
\nu_{i}
of
\sigma_{i}
supported a union of the pleating loci of
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{i}
.

For each
i
, the difference
\nu_{i+1}-\nu_{i}
of the transverse cocycles is supported on the geodesics corresponding to the edges of the tetrahedron, so that, on the surface, the edges form an ideal rectangle with both diagonals.
Let
\mu_{i}
be the difference cocycle
\nu_{i+1}-\nu_{i}
.
From each vertex of the ideal rectangle, there are three leaves of
\nu_{i+1}-\nu_{i}
starting, and the sum of their weights is zero by Euclidean geometry (Figure
26
).

\begin{overpic}[Figure/FlipAndBendingAngles] \put(45.0,6.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$\sigma_{i}$}}
\put(35.0,16.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\sigma_{i+1}$}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 26.
The link of a vertex of the ideal rectangle —– the sum of the indicated singed angles is zero.

Note that
P_{X}
can be identified with
\sigma_{X}
by collapsing each horizontal edge of
P_{X}
to a point.
Hence, for every
i
, if
\alpha
is a closed curve on
P_{X}
or an arc connecting vertical edges of
P_{X}
, then
\mu_{i}(\alpha)=0
.
By regarding
\nu_{j}
is a geodesic lamination on
\sigma_{X}
, their union

\cup_{j=1}^{i}\nu_{j}
is a graph on
\sigma_{X}
whose vertices are the transversal intersection points of the triangulations.
A small regular neighborhood
N
of
\cup_{j=1}^{i}\nu_{j}
is decomposed into a small regular neighborhood
N_{0}
of the vertices and a small regular neighborhood of the edges minus
N_{0}
in
N\setminus N_{0}
.

Since, after the Whitehead moves, the pleated surface
\sigma_{X}
is transformed into a pleated surface
\sigma_{Y}
.
Thus
\nu_{n}-\nu_{Y}
gives a
\mathbb{Z}
-valued transversal cocycle.

By the construction of the regular homotopy, we have the following.

Proposition 9.15

(Train tracks for non-transversal branches)
.

For every non-transversally compatible branches
\mathcal{P}_{X}
of
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
and
\mathcal{P}_{Y}
of
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
,
there is a
\mathbb{Z}
-weighted immersed train-track graph
\Gamma_{P_{X}}
representing the transversal cocycle supported on
\cup_{j=1}^{i}\nu_{j}
.
Moreover, the train-track cocycle is independent of the choice of the flip sequence
w_{i}
.

Proof.

Given two flip sequences
(w_{i}),(w_{j}^{\prime})
connecting the triangulations of
\sigma_{X}
to
\sigma_{Y}
, there are connected by a sequence of sequences
(v_{i}^{k})
of triangulations connecting
\sigma_{X}
to
\sigma_{Y}
, such that
(v_{i}^{k})
and
(v_{i}^{k+1})
differ by either an involutivity, a commutativity or a pentagon relation (
[
Pen12
, Chapter 5, Corollary 1.2]
).
Clearly, the difference by an involutivity and a commutativity do not affect the resulting cocycle.
Also by the pentagon relation, the pleated surface does not change including the bending measure since each flip preserves the total bending along the vertices.
Therefore
(v_{i}^{k})
and
(v_{i}^{k+1})
give the same train-track cocycle.
∎

Therefore we obtain
\mathsf{A}\to\mathbb{Z}
.
By continuity and the connectedness in Lemma
9.14
. we have the following.

Corollary 9.16
.

[\Gamma_{P}]
is independent on the choice of the perturbation in Lemma
9.14
.

There are only finitely many combinatorial types of the train-tracks
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
.
We say that a branch
B_{X}
of
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
and a branch
B_{X}^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho^{\prime}}
are
isotopic
if they are diffeomorphic and isotopic on
S
.
Then there are only finitely many isotopy classes of branches of
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
for all
\rho\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\setminus K
.
Let
\alpha
be an arc
\alpha
on a branch
B_{X}
, such that each endpoint of
\alpha
is at either on a vertical edge or a vertex of
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
.

Proposition 9.17
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathsf{T}
.
For every
\epsilon>0
, there a compact subset
K
in
\textstyle\chi
with the following property:
For every pair
(B_{X},\alpha)
of an isotopy class of a branch
B_{X}
and an arc
\alpha
as above, there is a constant
k_{\alpha}>0
such that, if
B_{X}
is a (non-transversal) branch of
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
for some
\rho\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}\setminus K
, then,
2\pi[\Gamma_{P_{X}}](\alpha)
is
(1+\epsilon,k_{\alpha})
-quasi-isometric to
\sqrt{2}(V_{X}|P_{X}-V_{Y}|P_{Y})(\alpha)
.

Proof.

Since the length of the flip sequence is bounded from above, the difference between
\nu_{X}
and
\nu_{n}
is uniformly bounded in the space of transverse cocycles on
S
.
Then the assertion follows.
∎

9.4.5.
Independency of the transverse cocycle

From the train-track decompositions
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
and
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
, we have constructed a weighted train-track graph
\Gamma_{\rho}
(
Theorem

9.8
(
1
)).
Next we show its cocycle is independent on the train-track decompositions
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
and
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
(
Theorem

9.8
(
2
)).

Recall that the train-track decompositions
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
and
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
are determined by

(1)

the holonomy equivariant circle system
{\bf c}=\{c_{h}\}
indexed by horizontal edges
h
of
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{X,\rho}
(given by Lemma
7.3
),

(2)

the realization
W_{Y}
of
[V_{Y,\rho}]_{X,\rho}
on
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
(Lemma
6.7
), and

(3)

the choice of vertical edges of
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
(Theorem
8.6
(
2
)).

Proposition 9.18
.

The cocycle
[\Gamma_{\rho}]\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathbb{Z}
constructed above is independent of the construction for
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
and
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
, i.e. (
1
), (
2
), (
3
).

Proof.

(
1
)
By Proposition
7.4
, given two appropriate circle systems
\{c_{h}\}
and
\{c^{\prime}_{h}\}
, there is an equivariant isotopy of circles systems
\{c_{t,h}\}
connecting
\{c_{h}\}
to
\{c^{\prime}_{h}\}
.
Then accordingly, we obtain a continuous family of cocycles
[\Gamma_{t,\rho}]\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathbb{Z}
.
As it takes discrete values,
[\Gamma_{t,\rho}]
must remain the same.

By the different choices for (
2
) and (
3
), the
\mathbb{Z}
-weights on
\Gamma
shift across bigon regions corresponding vertical edges of
T_{Y,\rho}
by integer values.
These weight shifts clearly preserve the cocycle
[\Gamma_{\rho}]\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

9.4.6.
Continuity of the transverse cocycle

Next we prove the continuity of
[\Gamma_{\rho}]
in
\rho
claimed in (
3
).

Definition 9.19

(Convergence and semi-convergence of train tracks)
.

Suppose that
C_{i}\in\mathsf{P}
converges to
C\in\mathsf{P}
.
In addition, suppose, for each
i
, there are a train-track structure
\mathcal{T}_{i}
of
C_{i}
and a train-track structure
\mathcal{T}
for
C
.
Then,

•

\mathcal{T}_{i}

converges
to
\mathcal{T}
if

–

for every branch
\mathcal{P}
of
\mathcal{T}
, there is a sequence of branches
\mathcal{P}_{i}
of
\mathcal{T}_{i}
converging to
\mathcal{P}
, and

–

for every sequence of branches
\mathcal{P}_{i}
of
\mathcal{T}_{i}
, up to a subsequence, converges to either a branch of
\mathcal{T}
or an edge of a branch of
\mathcal{T}
.

•

\mathcal{T}_{i}

semi-converges
to
\mathcal{T}
if there is a subdivision of
\mathcal{T}
into another circular train-track structure
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
so that
\mathcal{T}_{i}
converges to
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 9.20
.

Let
\rho_{i}
be a sequence in
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
converging to
\rho\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}\setminus K
, where
K
is a sufficiently large compact (as in
Theorem

8.6
).
Pick an equivariant circle system
{\bf c}_{i}
for
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho_{i}}
by Lemma
7.3
which converges to a circle system
{\bf c}
for
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
.
Then, up to a subsequence,

•

the circular train track
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho_{i}}
of
C_{X,\rho_{i}}
semi-converges to a circular train track
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
of
C_{X,\rho}
;

•

the circular train track
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho_{i}}
of
C_{Y,\rho_{i}}
semi-converges to a circular train track
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
of
C_{Y,\rho}
;

•

\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
is semi-compatible with
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

6.19
,
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho_{i}}
semi-converges to
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
.
Therefore
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho_{i}}
converges to a subdivision
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
as
i\to\infty
.
Then, if
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}\neq\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}^{\prime}
, then
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
is obtained from
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}^{\prime}
by gluing non-rectangular branches with rectangular branches of small width or replacing long rectangles into spiral cylinders (as in §
5.3
and §
6.7
).

Recall that7 the realization of
[V_{Y,\rho_{i}}]_{X,\rho_{i}}
in the train track
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho_{i}}
is unique up to shifting across vertical edges of non-rectangular branches (Proposition
6.12
(
2
)).
Therefore, up to a subsequence, the realization of
[V_{Y,\rho_{i}}]_{X,\rho_{i}}
on
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho_{i}}
converges to a realization of
[V_{Y,\rho}]_{X}
on
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}^{\prime}
,
Since
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}^{\prime}
is a subdivision of
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
, the limit can be regarded as also a realization on
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
.
Since the realization determines the train-track structure of
E_{Y,\rho}
, up to a subsequence,
\mathbf{T}_{Y,\rho_{i}}
converges to a bounded train-track
\mathbf{T}_{Y,\rho}^{\prime}
.
Then
\mathbf{T}_{Y,\rho}
is transformed to
\mathbf{T}_{Y,\rho}^{\prime}
by possibly sliding vertical edges and subdividing spiral cylinders to wide rectangles.
Moreover, by Theorem
8.2
(
1
),
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
is
(1+\epsilon,\epsilon)
-quasiisometric to
\mathbf{T}_{Y,\rho}
.
Therefore, up to a subsequence,
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho_{i}}
converges to a circular train-track structure
\mathcal{T}_{Y}^{\prime}
of
C_{Y,\rho}
.
If
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
is different from
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
can be transformed to
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}^{\prime}
by sliding vertical edges and subdividing spiral cylinders into rectangles.

By Theorem
8.6
,
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho_{i}}
is additively
2\pi
-close to
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho_{i}}
in the Hausdorff metric of
E^{1}_{X,\rho}
.
Therefore, up to a subsequence
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho_{i}}
converges to a circular train track decomposition
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}^{\prime}
semi-diffeomorphic to
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
.
Moreover
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
can be transformed to
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}^{\prime}
possibly by subdividing and sliding by
2\pi
or
4\pi
.

We have already shown that
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
is semi-diffeomorphic to
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
(Theorem
8.6
).

9.20

Finally we have the continuity (
3
).

Corollary 9.21
.

[\Gamma_{\rho_{i}}]\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathbb{Z}
converges to
[\Gamma_{\rho}]\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathbb{Z}
as
i\to\infty
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho_{i}}
semi-converges to
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
,
up to taking a subsequence,
\mathcal{T}_{X,i}
converges to a subdivision
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{X,\rho}
of
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
.
Accordingly, there is a subdivision
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}
of
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
, such that, to up a subsequence,
\mathcal{T}_{Y,i}
converges to
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}
and that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{X,\rho}
is semi-diffeomorphic to
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}
.

Let
\Gamma_{\rho_{i}}
be the
\mathbb{Z}
-weighted train-track given by
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho_{i}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho_{i}}
.
Let
\Gamma_{\rho}^{\prime}
be the
\mathbb{Z}
-weighted train track given
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{X,\rho}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}
.
Then, by the convergence of the train tracks,
\Gamma_{\rho_{i}}
converges to
\Gamma_{\rho}^{\prime}
as
i\to\infty
.
Since
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{X,\rho}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{Y,\rho}
are obtained by sliding and subdividing
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
and
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
respectively, thus
\Gamma_{\rho}^{\prime}
and
\Gamma_{\rho}
yield the same cocycle
\mathsf{C}\to\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

9.5.
Approximation of the grafting cocycle
[\Gamma_{\rho}]
by vertical foliations

Suppose that
X,Y
be distinct marked Riemann surfaces homeomorphic to
S
such that
X
and
Y
have the same orientation.
For a branch
B_{X}
of
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
, let
\mathsf{A}(B_{X})
be the homotopy class of arcs
\alpha
on
\mathcal{R}_{X}
such that every endpoint of
\alpha
is either on a vertical edge or a vertex of
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}.

Theorem 9.22
.

Let
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}
be essential closed curves on
S
.
Then, for every
\epsilon>0
, there is a bounded subset
K_{\epsilon}
of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
such that, for every
\rho\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}\setminus K
,
the grafting cocycle
[\Gamma_{\rho}]
times
2\pi
is
(1+\epsilon,q)
-quasi-isometric to
\sqrt{2}(V_{X,\rho}-V_{Y,\rho})
along
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}
. That is,

(2)

(1-\epsilon)2\pi\Gamma_{\rho}(c_{i})-q<\sqrt{2}(V_{X,\rho}(c_{i})-V_{Y,\rho}(c_{i}))<(1+\epsilon)2\pi\Gamma_{\rho}(c_{i})+q

for all
i=1,2,\dots,n
.

Proof.

Let
H\in\mathsf{PML}
.
Recall that
E^{1}_{X,H}
is the flat surface conformal to
X
, such that the horizontal foliation is
H
and
{\rm Area}E^{1}(X,H)=1
.
Let
\mathbf{T}_{X,H}
be the bounded train-track structure of
E_{X,H}
.

Then, every closed curve
c
can be isotoped to a closed curve
c^{\prime}
so that, for each branch of
B
of
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
,
c^{\prime}|B
is an arc connecting different vertices.
Let
c^{\prime}_{1},\dots,c^{\prime}_{m}
be the decomposition into sub-arcs.
By the finiteness of possible train-tracks, the number
m
of the subarcs is bounded from above for all
\rho
.
Then
2\pi\Gamma_{\rho}|c^{\prime}_{j}
is, if
B
is a transversal branch,
(1+\epsilon,\epsilon)
-quasi-isometric to
\sqrt{2}(V_{X,\rho}|B-V_{Y,\rho}|B)c_{k}^{\prime}
by Proposition
9.12
(
4
),
Proposition

9.11
, Proposition
9.10
, and, if non-transversal,
(1+\epsilon,q)
-quasi-isometric by Proposition
9.17
.
As the number of subarcs is bounded, the assertion follows.
∎

10.
The discreteness

10.1.
The discreteness of the intersection of holonomy varieties

Theorem 10.1
.

Suppose that
X,Y
are marked Riemann surface structures on
S
with the same orientation.
Then
every (connected) component of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
is bounded.

Proof.

Let
K
be a component of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
.
Suppose, to the contrary, that
V
is unbounded in
\textstyle\chi
.
Then, there is a path
\rho_{t}
in
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
which leaves every compact subset.
Then, by
Corollary

4.6
, by taking a diverging sequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
, there are
k_{i},k_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
\frac{k_{i}}{k_{i}^{\prime}}\to 1
as
i\to\infty
and

\lim_{i\to\infty}k_{i}H_{X,\rho_{t_{i}}}=\lim_{i\to\infty}k^{\prime}_{i}H_{Y,\rho_{t_{i}}}\in\mathsf{ML}.

By taking a subsequence, we may, in addition, assume that their vertical foliations
[V_{X,\rho_{t_{i}}}]
and
[V_{Y,\rho_{t_{i}}}]
converge in
\mathsf{PML}
.
Thus let
[V_{X,\infty}]
and
[V_{Y,\infty}]
be their respective limits in
\mathsf{PML}
.
Since
X\neq Y
,
V_{X,\infty}
and
V_{Y,\infty}
can not be asymptotically the same, in comparison to their horizontal foliations.
Then
V_{X,\rho_{t_{i}}}-V_{Y,\rho_{t_{i}}}
“diverges to
\infty
”.
That is, there is a closed curve
\alpha
on
S
, such that

|V_{X,\rho_{t_{i}}}(\alpha)-V_{Y,\rho_{t_{i}}}(\alpha)|\to\infty

as
i\to\infty
.

Let
[\Gamma_{\rho_{t}}]\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathbb{Z}
be the function given by Theorem
9.8
.
As
[\Gamma_{\rho_{t}}]
is continuous (Theorem
9.8
(
3
)),
[\Gamma_{\rho_{t}}]\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathbb{Z}
is a constant function (for
t\gg 0
).
On the other hand, by Theorem
9.22
, there is
q>0
such that

\sqrt{2}(V_{X,\rho_{t_{i}}}-V_{Y,\rho_{t_{i}}})(\alpha)
is
(1+\epsilon_{i},q)
-quasi-isometrically close to
2\pi[\Gamma_{\rho_{t_{i}}}](\alpha)
, and
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
.
We thus obtain a contradiction.

10.1

Since
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}
and
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}
are complex analytic, thus their intersection is also a complex analytic set (Theorem 5.4 in
[
FG02
]
).
Therefore, since every bounded connected analytic set is a singleton (Proposition
2.7
), Theorem
10.1
implies the following.

Corollary 10.2
.

{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
is a discrete set.

We will, moreover, show that this intersection is non-empty in §
12
.

10.2.
A weak simultaneous uniformization theorem

In this section, using Corollary
10.2
, we prove a weak version of a simultaneous uniformization theorem for general representations.
Let
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
be any non-elementary representation which lifts to
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
.
Let
C,D
be
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures on
S^{+}
with the holonomy
\rho
.
Then, if a neighborhood
U_{\rho}
of
\rho
in
\textstyle\chi
is sufficiently small, then there are (unique) neighborhoods
V_{C}
and
W_{D}
of
C
and
D
in
\mathsf{P}
, respectively, which are biholomorphic to
U_{\rho}
by
\operatorname{Hol}\colon\mathsf{P}\to{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
.
Then, for every
\eta\in U_{\rho}
, there are unique
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures
C_{\eta}
in
V_{C}
and
D_{\eta}
in
W_{D}
with holonomy
\eta
.
Let
\Phi_{\rho,U}=\Phi\colon U_{\rho}\to\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{T}
be the map which takes
\eta\in U_{\rho}
to the pair of the marked Riemann surface structures of
C_{\eta}
and
D_{\eta}
.

Theorem 10.3
.

\Phi_{\rho,U}
is a finite-to-one open mapping.

Proof.

By Corollary
10.2
, the fiber of
\Phi
is discrete.
In addition,
\Phi
is holomorphic and
\dim U_{\rho}=2\dim\mathsf{T}
.
Therefore, by Theorem
2.8
,
\Phi
is an open map.
∎

11.
Opposite orientations

In this section, when the orientations of the Riemann surfaces are opposite, we show the discreteness of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
analogous to Theorem
11.1
and the local uniformization theorem analogous to Theorem
10.3
.

Theorem 11.1
.

Fix
X\in\mathsf{T}
and
Y\in\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
.
Then,
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
is a non-empty discrete set.

Since the proof is similar to the case when the orientations coincide, we simply outline the proof, yet explain how some parts are modified.
We leave the details to the reader.

Recall that we have constructed compatible train track decomposition regardless of the orientation of
XY
(§
8.4
, §
8.5
).
In summary, we have the following (in the case of opposite orientataions):

Proposition 11.2
.

Fix
X\in\mathsf{T}
and
Y\in\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
.
For every
\epsilon>0
, there is a bounded subset
K_{\epsilon}
in
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
, such that,
if
\rho\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}\setminus K_{\epsilon}
, then
there are circular polygonal train-track decompositions
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
of
C_{X,\rho}
and
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
of
C_{Y,\rho}
, such that

•

\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
and
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
are semi-diffeomorphic, and

•

\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
and
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
are
(1+\epsilon,\epsilon)
-quasi-isometric to the train track decompositions
\mathbf{T}_{X,\rho}
of the flat surface
E_{X,\rho}^{1}
and
\mathbf{T}_{Y,\rho}
of the flat surface
E_{Y,\rho}^{1}
, respectively, with respect to the normalized metrics.

In the case when the orientation of
X
and
Y
are the same, in Theorem
9.8
, we constructed a
\mathbb{Z}
-weighted train-track graph representing the “difference” of projective structures on
X
and
Y
with the same holonomy.
As the orientations of
X
and
Y
are different, we shall construct a
\mathbb{Z}
-weighted train track graph representing, in this case, the “sum” of the
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures on
X
and
Y
with the same holonomy.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
and
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
be circular train track decompositions of
C_{X,\rho}
and
C_{Y,\rho}
given by Proposition.

11.2

Let
\{h_{X,1},h_{X_{2}},\dots,h_{X_{n}}\}
be the horizontal edges of
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
.
Similarly to §
9.4
, we first define the
\mathbb{Z}
-valued function on the set of horizontal edges.
For each
i=1,\dots,n
, let
c_{i}
be the round circle on
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
supporting the development of
h_{X,i}
.
First, suppose that
h_{X,i}
corresponds to an edge
h_{Y,i}
of
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
by the collapsing map
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}\to\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
.
Since
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
and
\mathcal{T}_{Y,\rho}
are compatible, the corresponding endpoints of
h_{X,i}
and
h_{Y,i}
map to the same point on
c_{i}
by their developing maps.
Thus, by identifying the endpoints, we obtain a covering map from a circle
h_{X,i}\cup h_{Y,i}
onto
c_{i}
.
Then, define
\gamma_{X,i}(h_{X,i})\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
to be the covering degree.

Next, suppose that
h_{X,i}
corresponds to a vertex of
\mathcal{T}_{Y,i}
.
Then the endpoints of
h_{X,i}
develop to the same point on
c_{i}
.
The circle obtained by identifying endpoints of
h_{X,i}
covers
c_{i}
.
Thus, let
\gamma_{X,i}(h_{X,i})\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
be the covering degree.

Similarly to §
9.4
, we shall construct a
\mathbb{Z}
-weighted train-track graph
\Gamma_{\rho}
immersed in
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
.
On each branch
\mathcal{P}_{X}
of
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
,
we construct a
\mathbb{Z}
-weighted train-track graph
\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{X}}
on
\mathcal{P}_{X}
such that, for every
\epsilon>0
, there is a compact subset
K
of
\textstyle\chi
satisfying the following:

•

The endpoints of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{X}}
are on horizontal edges of
\mathcal{P}_{X}
.

•

They agree with
\gamma_{X,\rho}
along the horizontal edges.

•

If
\mathcal{P}_{X}
is a transversal branch, then, for
\rho\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}\setminus K
, then
2\pi\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{X}}(\alpha)
is
(1+\epsilon,\epsilon)
-quasi-isometric to
\sqrt{2}(V_{X,\rho}|P_{X})(\alpha)+\sqrt{2}(V_{Y,\rho}|P_{Y})(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P}_{X})
.

•

For every smooth isotopy class of a staircase surface
B
on a flat surface homeomorphic to
S
and every arc
\alpha
on
B
connecting points on horizontal edges or vertices, there is a positive constant
q(B,\alpha)
such that, if
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
contains a non-transversal branch
\mathcal{B}_{X}
smoothly isotopic to
B
on
S
, then

2\pi\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}_{X}}(\alpha)
is
(1+\epsilon,q(B,\alpha))
-quasi-isometric to
\sqrt{2}\,V_{X,\rho}|B_{X}(\alpha)+\sqrt{2}\,V_{Y,\rho}|B_{Y}(\alpha)
.

Theorem 11.3
.

Let
X\in\mathsf{T}
and
Y\in\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
.
For every
\epsilon>0
, there is a bounded subset
K_{\epsilon}\subset{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
such that,
for every
\rho\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}\setminus K_{\epsilon}
, there is a
\mathbb{Z}
-weighted graph
\Gamma_{\rho}
carried in
\mathcal{T}_{X,\rho}
such that

(1)

the induced cocycle
[\Gamma_{\rho}]\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathbb{Z}
changes continuously in
\rho\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}\setminus K
,

(2)

for every loop
\alpha
on
S
, there is
q_{\alpha}>0
, such that
2\pi\Gamma_{\rho}(\alpha)
is
(1+\epsilon,q_{\alpha})
-quasi-isometric to
\sqrt{2}(V_{X,\rho}(\alpha)+V_{Y,\rho}(\alpha))
for all
\rho\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}\setminus K
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to
Theorem

9.8
(
3
) and Theorem
9.22
.∎

Then, Theorem
11.3
implies, similarly to Theorem
10.1
, the following:

Theorem 11.4
.

Each connected component of

{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
is bounded.

12.
The completeness

In this section, we prove the completeness in
A
.
Let
Q
be a connected component of the Bers’ space
\mathsf{B}
; then
Q
is a complex submanifold of
(\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast})\times(\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast})
, and
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Q=6g-6
.
We call that
\psi\colon\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast}\to\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
is the uniformization map and
\Psi\colon Q\to(\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast})^{2}
is defined by
\Psi(C,D)=(\psi(C),\psi(D))
.
Then, by Theorem
10.3
,
\Psi
is an open holomorphic map.
In this section, we prove the completeness of
\Psi
.

Lemma 12.1
.

The open map
\Psi\colon Q\to(\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast})^{2}
has a
local path lifting property
.
That is, for every
z\in Q
, there is a neighborhood
W
of
\Psi(z)
such that if path
\alpha_{t},\,0\leq t\leq 1
in
W
satisfies
\zeta(z)=\alpha_{0}
, then there is a lift
\tilde{\alpha}_{t}
of
\alpha_{t}
to
Q
with
\tilde{\alpha}_{0}=z
.

Proof.

Since
\Psi
is an open map and
\dim Q=\dim(\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast})^{2}
,
\Psi
is a locally branched covering map.
Then, for every
z\in Q
, there is an open neighborhood
V
of
z
in
Q
and a finite group
G_{z}
biholomorphically acting
V
, such that
\Psi
is
G_{z}
-invariant, and
\Psi
induces the biholomorphic map
V/G_{z}\to\Psi(V)
.

For
g\in G_{z}\setminus\{id\}
, let
F_{g}\subset W
be the (pointwise) fixed point set of
g
.
Clearly
F_{g}
is a proper analytic subset, and thus
F\coloneqq\cup_{g\in G_{z}\setminus\{id\}}F_{g}
is an analytic subset strictly contained in
W
.

For every path
\alpha\colon[0,1]\to V
with
\alpha(0)=\Psi(z)
,
we can take a one-parameter family of paths
\alpha_{t}\,(t\in[0,1])
in
W
with
\alpha_{t}(0)=\Psi(z)

such that
\alpha_{1}=\alpha
and, for
t<1
,
\alpha_{t}
is disjoint from
\Psi(F)
(since
\Psi(F)
has complex codimension, at least, one.)

Then, for
t<1
,
\alpha_{t}
continuously lifts a path
\tilde{\alpha}_{t}\colon[0,1]\to Q
, and
\alpha_{t}
converges to a desired lift of
\alpha_{1}
as
t\to 1
.
∎

Now we are ready to prove the completeness.

Theorem 12.2
.

\Psi\colon Q\to(\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast})^{2}\setminus\Delta
is a complete map, where
\Delta
is the diagonal set.

The completeness of Theorem
12.2
immediately implies the following:

Corollary 12.3
.

\Psi\colon Q\to(\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast})^{2}\setminus\Delta
is surjective onto a connected component of
(\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast})^{2}\setminus\Delta
.

Proof of Theorem
12.2
.

By Lemma
12.1
,
\Psi
has a local path lifting property.
Thus, suppose that
(X_{t},Y_{t})\colon[0,1]\to(\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast})^{2}\setminus\Delta\penalty\ (0\leq t\leq 1)
be a path and there is a (partial) lift
(C_{t},D_{t})\colon[0,1)\to Q
of the path
(X_{t},Y_{t})
.
For each
t\in[0,1]
, let
\rho_{t}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
denote the common holonomy of
C_{t}
and
D_{t}
.
By the continuity, the orientations of Riemann surfaces in the pairs obviously remain the same along
\rho_{t}
for all
t>0
.

First we, in addition, suppose that there is an increasing sequence
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots
converging to
1
, such that
\rho_{t_{i}}
converges to a representation in
\textstyle\chi
.
By Corollary
10.2
or, in the case of opposite orientations, Theorem
11.1
,
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X_{1}}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y_{1}}
is a discrete subset of
\textstyle\chi
.
Then, since
(X_{t},Y_{t})
converges to a point
(X_{1},Y_{1})
in
\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{T}
, and
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X_{t}}
and
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y_{t}}
change continuously in
t\in[0,1]
, every neighborhood
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X_{1}}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y_{1}}
contains
\rho_{t_{i}}
for all sufficiently large
i
.
Thus the sequence
\rho_{t_{i}}
converges to a point in
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X_{1}}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y_{1}}
.
Since
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X_{t}}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y_{t}}
is a discrete set in
\textstyle\chi
which continuously changes in
t\in[0,1]
,
we indeed have a genuine convergence.

Lemma 12.4
.

\rho_{t}
converges to
\rho_{1}
as
t\to 1
.

By this lemma,
(C_{t},D_{t})
converges in
(\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast})\times(\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast})
as
t\to 1
, so that the partial lift
(C_{t},D_{t})
extends to
t=1
.

Thus it is suffices to show the addition assumption always holds:

Proposition 12.5
.

There is a compact subset
K
in
\textstyle\chi
, such that, for every
t>0
, there is
t^{\prime}>0
such that
\rho_{t^{\prime}}\in K
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as the proof of
Theorem
10.1
or Theorem
11.1
, which states that each component of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
is a bounded subset of
\textstyle\chi
.

For
0\leq t<1
,
let
V_{C_{t}}
and
V_{D_{t}}
be the vertical measured foliations of
C_{t}
and
D_{t}
, respectively.
Suppose, to the contrary, that
\rho_{t}
leaves every compact subset of
\textstyle\chi
.
As
(X_{t},Y_{t})
converges to
(X,Y)\in(\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast})^{2}\setminus\Delta
and
\operatorname{Hol}(C_{t})=\operatorname{Hol}(D_{t})
, similarly to Theorem
9.8
or
Theorem

11.3
, for
t
close to
1
, we can construct a
\mathbb{Z}
-weighed train track
\Gamma_{t}
on
S
, such that

•

the intersection function
[\Gamma_{t}]\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathbb{Z}
is continuous in
t
, and

•

for every closed curve
\alpha
on
S
, there is a constant
q_{\alpha}>0
and a function
\epsilon_{t}>0
converging to
0
, such that, for all sufficiently large
t>0
,
[\Gamma_{t}](\alpha)
is
(1+\epsilon_{t},q_{\alpha})
-quasi-isometric to
V_{C_{t}}(\alpha)-V_{D_{t}}(\alpha)
if the orientation of
X
and
Y
are the same and to
V_{C_{t}}(\alpha)+V_{D_{t}}(\alpha)
if the orientation of
X
and
Y
are different.

The first condition implies that the intersection number is constant in
t
, whereas the second condition implies that the intersection number with some closed curve
\alpha
diverges to infinity as
t\to 1
.
This is a contradiction.
∎

12.2

Last we remark the behavior of
\operatorname{Hol}
near the diagonal
\Delta
.

Proposition 12.6
.

Let
(C_{t},D_{t}),\,0\leq t<1
be a path in
\mathsf{B}
, such that
\Psi(C_{t},D_{t})
converges to a diagonal point
(X,X)
of
(\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast})^{2}
.
Then
\operatorname{Hol}C_{t}=\operatorname{Hol}D_{t}
leaves every compact set in
\textstyle\chi
as
t\to 1
.

Proof.

Fix arbitrary
X\in\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
and a bounded open subset
U
in
\chi
.
Recall that
\operatorname{Hol}\colon\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast}\to{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}
is a locally biholomorphic map,
and that, for all
Y\in\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
, the set
\mathsf{P}_{Y}
of
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structures on
Y
is properly embedded in
\chi
by
\operatorname{Hol}
.
Therefore, if an open neighborhood
V
of
X
in
\mathsf{T}\sqcup\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
is sufficiently small, then, letting
\mathsf{P}_{V}
denotes all
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
-structure on Riemann surfaces in
V
,
\mathsf{P}_{V}\cap\operatorname{Hol}^{-1}(U)
embeds into
U
by the holonomy map
\operatorname{Hol}
.
In particular, for every
Y,Z\in V
,
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Z}
is disjoint from
U
.
Then the assertion is immediate.
∎

12.1.
Cardinalities of the intersections

By the surjectivity of
Corollary

12.3
and the existence of non-quasi-Fuchsian components of
\mathsf{B}
in
\mathsf{P}\sqcup\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
in
Lemma

2.6
, we immediately have the following:

Corollary 12.7
.

Let
X,Y
be distinct marked Riemann surface structures on
S
with any orientations.
If the orientations of
X
and
Y
are opposite, the intersection
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
contains, at least, two points, if the orientations of
X
and
Y
are the same,
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{X}\cap{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}_{Y}
contains, at least, one point.

13.
A proof of the simultaneous uniformization theorem

In this section, using Theorem
A
, we give a new proof of the simultaneous uniformization theorem without using the measurable Riemann mapping theorem.
Recall
\mathsf{QF}
is the quasi-Fuchsian space, and it is embedded in
\mathsf{B}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Given
(C,D)
in
\mathsf{QF}
, the universal covers
\tilde{C},\tilde{D}
are the connected components of
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
minus its equivariant Jordan curve equivariant via
\operatorname{Hol}C=\operatorname{Hol}D
.

Lemma 13.1
.

\mathsf{QF}
is a union of connected components of
\mathsf{B}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Proof.

As being a quasi-isometric embedding is an open condition,
\mathsf{QF}
is an open subset of
\mathsf{B}
.
Thus, it suffices to show that
\mathsf{QF}
is closed.

Let
(C_{i},D_{i})\in\mathsf{P}\times\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
be a sequence in
\mathsf{QF}
which converges to
(C,D)\in\mathsf{P}\times\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
.
Let
\rho_{i}\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
be the quasi-Fuchsian representation of
C_{i}
and
D_{i}
.
We show that the holonomy
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
of the limits
C
and
D
is also quasi-Fuchsian.
Let
\tilde{C}_{i}
and
\tilde{D}_{i}
be the universal covers of
C_{i}
and
D_{i}
, respectively.
Then
\tilde{C}_{i}
and
\tilde{D}_{i}
are the components of
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
minus the
\rho_{i}
-equivariant Jordan curve.
Let
f_{i}\colon\tilde{C_{i}}\cup\mathbb{S}^{1}\to{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
and
g_{i}\colon\tilde{D_{i}}\cup\mathbb{S}^{1}\to{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
be the extensions of the embeddings to their boundary circles by a theorem of Carathéodory.
Let
h_{i}
be the homeomorphism
\tilde{C}_{i}\cup\mathbb{S}^{1}\cup\tilde{D}_{i}\to{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
.

Since embeddings
\operatorname{dev}C_{i}
converge to
\operatorname{dev}C
uniformly on compact as
i\to\infty
, the limit
\operatorname{dev}C
is also an embedding.
(Suppose, to the contrary, that
\operatorname{dev}C\colon\tilde{C}\to{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
is not embedding.
Then there are distinct open subsets in
\tilde{C}
which homeomorphically map onto the same open subset in
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
. Then
\operatorname{dev}C_{i}
is also not embedding for all sufficiently large
i
against the assumption.)
Thus, by the convergence of corresponding convex pleated surfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, the equivariant property implies that
\operatorname{dev}C
extends to the boundary circle continuously and equivariantly.
Similarly, since the embedding
\operatorname{dev}D_{i}
converges to
\operatorname{dev}D
, then
\operatorname{dev}D
is also an embedding, and
\operatorname{dev}D
extends to the boundary circle continuously and equivariantly.
Therefore
h_{i}
converges to a continuous map

h\colon\mathbb{S}^{2}\cong\tilde{C}\cup\mathbb{S}^{1}\cup\tilde{D}\to{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}

such that the restriction of
h
to
\tilde{C}\sqcup\tilde{D}
is an embedding.

The domain and the target of
h
are both homeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
Therefore, if
h|\mathbb{S}^{1}
is not a Jordan curve on
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
, then there is a point
z\in{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
, such that
h^{-1}(z)
is a single segment of
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
By the equivariant property,
h^{-1}(z)=\mathbb{S}^{1}
, and
{\rm Im}\,h
is a wedge of two copies of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, which is a contradiction.
∎

The following asserts that the diagonal of
\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
corresponds to the Fuchsian representations.

Lemma 13.2
.

Let
X\in\mathsf{T}
.
Let
\eta\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
be a quasi-Fuchsian representation, such that the ideal boundary of
\mathbb{H}^{3}/{\rm Im}\rho
realizes the marked Riemann surface
X
and its complex conjugate
X^{\ast}\in\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
.
Then
\eta
is the Fuchsian representation
\pi_{1}(S)\to{\rm PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that
X=\mathbb{H}^{2}/{\rm Im}\eta
.

Proof.

By the Riemann uniformization theorem, the universal covers
\tilde{X}
and
\tilde{X}^{\ast}
are the upper and the lower half planes.
Then, by identifying their ideal boundaries equivariantly, we obtain
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
so that the universal covers
\tilde{X}
and
\tilde{X}^{\ast}
are round open disks.
Let
(C,D)\in\mathsf{P}\times\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
be the pair corresponding to
\eta
, such that the complex structure of
C
is
X
and the complex structure of
D
is on
X^{\ast}
.

On the other hand, the universal covers
\tilde{C}
and
\tilde{D}
are connected components of
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}\setminus\Lambda(\eta)
, where
\Lambda(\eta)
is the
\eta
-equivariant Jordan curve in
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
.
Thus, there is a
\eta
-equivariant homeomorphism
\phi\colon{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}\to{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
, such that

\phi
restricts to a biholomorphism from
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}\setminus{\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}}\to{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}\setminus\Lambda(\eta)
.

Then, by Morera’s theorem for the line integral along triangles (see
[
SS03
]
for example),
\phi
is a genuine biholomorphic map
{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}\to{\mathbb{C}{\rm P}}^{1}
.
Therefore
\phi
is a Möbius transformation, and therefore
\eta
is conformally conjugate to the Fuchsian representation uniformizing
X
.
∎

Proposition 13.3
.

\mathsf{QF}
is a single connected component of
\mathsf{B}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
13.1
,
\mathsf{QF}
is the union of some connected components of
\mathsf{B}
.
By Theorem
A
, for every component
Q
of
\mathsf{QF}
, the image
\Psi(Q)
contains the diagonal
\{(X,X^{\ast})\}
of
\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
.
Then, by Lemma
13.2
, every diagonal pair
(X,X^{\ast})\in\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
corresponds to a unique point in
\mathsf{QF}
.
Therefore
\mathsf{QF}
is connected.
∎

Last we reprove the simultaneous uniformization theorem.

Theorem 13.4
.

\mathsf{QF}
is biholomorphic to
\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
by
\Psi
.

Proof.

By Theorem
A
,
\Psi
is a complete local branched covering map.
Since
\Psi
is surjective,
by Lemma
13.2
,
\Psi
is a degree-one over the diagonal
\{(X,\overline{X})\mid X\in\mathsf{T}\}
, and the diagonal corresponds to the Fuchsian space.

The set of ramification points of
\Psi
is an analytic set.
The Fuchsian space is a totally real subspace of dimension
6g-6
.
Therefore, if the ramification locus contains the Fuchsian space, then the locus must have the complex dimension
6g-6
, the full dimension.
This is a contradiction as
\Psi
is a locally branched covering map.
Therefore,
\mathsf{QF}\to\mathsf{T}\times\mathsf{T}^{\ast}
has degree one, and thus it is biholomorphic.
∎
